2026 年 10 月 23 日 全6 頁

Indicators Update 
2026 年 9 月全国消費者物価 
サービスや非耐久財で価格上昇、コアCPI は9 カ月ぶり＋2％台

経済調査部 エコノミスト 横田 凱

[要約] 
◼ 2026 年 9 月の全国コアCPI（除く生鮮食品）は前年比＋2.1％と前月から伸び率が拡大
し、9 カ月ぶりに 2％台となった。生鮮食品やエネルギーを除いた全国新コアコア CPI
は同＋2.4％と、こちらも前月から伸び率が拡大し、6 カ月ぶりに 2％台に達した。食
料品、旅行関連、紙製品などの値上げと、政策要因の剥落が重なり、サービスや非耐
久消費財（除く生鮮食品、エネルギー）の伸び率が高まった。 
◼ コア CPI の前年比の動きを財・サービス別に見ると、サービス、非耐久消費財（除く
生鮮食品、エネルギー） 、耐久消費財、半耐久消費の伸び率が拡大し、エネルギーのマ
イナス幅が縮小した。 
◼ 先行きの物価上昇率について、コア CPI 上昇率は 26 年度末にかけて前年比＋3％超ま
で高まると見込んでいる。原油高が、エネルギーにとどまらず、幅広い財・サービス
の価格へ波及するとみられる。円安や、賃上げの価格転嫁と併せ、基調的な物価への
上昇圧力となるだろう。27 年度は、こうした物価上昇圧力は継続する一方で、飲食料
品にかかる消費税率の引き下げにより、コアCPI の上昇率は同＋1％台に縮小するとみ
られる。

### Page 2

1

2 / 6

26 年9 月 CPI：食料品の値上げや政策要因の剥落でコア CPI の伸び率は拡大 
2026 年9 月の全国コアCPI（除く生鮮食品）は前年比＋2.1％と前月から0.4％pt 伸び率が拡
大し、9 カ月ぶりに 2％台となった。また、全国新コアコア CPI（除く生鮮食品、エネルギー）
（同＋2.4％）も前月から0.5％pt 伸び率が拡大し、6 カ月ぶりに2％台に達した（図表1）。 
食料品、旅行関連、紙製品などの価格上昇と、政策要因の剥落が重なって、サービスや非耐
久消費財（除く生鮮食品、エネルギー）の伸び率が高まった。 
食料品については、コメ価格は下落基調にある一方で、他の品目で価格が上昇しており、生
鮮食品除く食料は前年比＋2.9％（8 月：同＋2.7％）と、14 カ月ぶりに伸び率が拡大した。旅
行関連は、大型連休が25 年9 月より長かったため需要が強く、価格を押し上げたとみられる。 
また、東京都の政策要因の剥落が全国の指数にも影響した。25 年 9 月に保育料無償化を拡充
したことの裏の影響や、水道料減免が終了したことで、コア指数の前年比が 0.1％pt 程度押し
上げられた。

食料品などの値上げや政策要因の剥落で、サービスや非耐久財が上昇 
コア CPI の前年比の動きを財・サービス別に見ると（図表 3）、サービス（前年比＋1.5％） 、
非耐久消費財（除く生鮮食品、エネルギー） （同＋3.4％） 、耐久消費財（同＋3.8％） 、半耐久
消費財（同＋3.1％）の伸び率が拡大し、エネルギー（同▲0.6％）のマイナス幅が縮小した。 
サービスでは、宿泊料（8 月：前年比▲1.4％→9 月：同＋4.6％）の伸び率がプラスに転じた
ほか、外国パック旅行費（8 月：同＋9.7％→9 月：同＋23.5％）のプラス幅が拡大した。これ
ら 2 品目だけでコア指数の前年比を 0.1％pt 程度押し上げた。また、保育所保育料（8 月：同
▲21.6％→9 月：同▲13.3％）のマイナス幅が縮小した。東京都が 25 年 9 月に無償化を拡充し
たことの裏の影響が出た。 
非耐久消費財（除く生鮮食品、エネルギー）では、水道料（8 月：前年比▲3.5％→9 月：同
＋4.9％）の伸び率がプラスに転じた 。東京都の水道料減免が終了したためだ。 食料品では、
冷凍ぎょうざ（8 月：同▲6.5％→9 月：同▲1.3％）などのマイナス幅が縮小した。また、トイ
レットペーパー（8 月：同＋10.5％→9 月：同＋17.8％）など、紙製品の伸び率が拡大した。 
耐久消費財では、冷蔵庫（8 月：前年比▲1.8％→9 月：同＋2.6％） 、パソコン（ノート型）
（8 月：同▲3.2％→9 月：同＋0.8％）などの伸び率がプラスに転じたほか、テレビ（8 月：同
＋10.7％→9 月：同＋14.8％）のプラス幅が拡大した。半導体価格高騰の影響が続いている。 
半耐久消費財では、ゴルフクラブ（8 月：前年比＋11.3％→9 月：同＋43.3％） 、自動車バッ
テリー（8 月：同＋1.4％→9 月：同＋7.0％） 、自動車タイヤ（8 月：同＋0.1％→9 月：同＋
1.4％）などの伸び率が拡大した。 
エネルギーでは、都市ガス代（8 月：前年比▲0.8％→9 月：同＋0.2％）の伸び率がプラスに
転じた。政府による補助金額は増加したが、資源高の影響を相殺するには至らなかった。電気
代やガソリンの前年比は前月から横ばいだった。

先行き：原油高などの波及で、コア CPI は26 年度末にかけて＋3％超を予想 
先行きの物価上昇率について、コア CPI 上昇率は 26 年度末にかけて前年比＋3％超まで高ま
るとみている。当面、原油高の幅広い財・サービスへの波及が物価を押し上げる主因となるだ
ろう。また、円安や、賃上げの価格転嫁も物価上昇につながるとみられる。 
原油価格の動向には注意が必要だ。米国とイスラエルが、米中間選挙後にイランへの攻撃を
再開する計画を策定したと伝えられる2など、中東の和平に向けた先行きは未だ不透明である。
原油価格の代表的な指標であるWTI 先物価格は 10 月 22 日終値で 99 ドル/バレル程度と、中東
情勢の悪化前よりも大幅に高い状況が続いている。さらに、代替調達の進展などを受け、輸入
原油価格は上昇が続くとみられる3。 
こうした原油高は、直接的にエネルギー価格を押し上げる。電気・ガスの燃料費調整制度で
は、3-5 カ月前のエネルギー資源の平均輸入価格が料金に反映されるため、原油高がラグを伴
って波及する。関連して、政府は9 月使用分（10 月請求分）まで電気・ガス料金の補助を実施
しているほか、27 年1-3 月にも同様の措置を検討していると報じられている4。しかし、25 年
夏、26 年冬にも同様の政策を実施しているため、前年比でみた物価上昇率の抑制効果は限定的
なものとなるだろう（巻末図表「エネルギー価格のコアCPI への影響とその見通し」）。また、
原油高は、エネルギー価格だけでなく、輸送費や原材料費の増加を通じて幅広い品目の価格に
も波及するため5、基調的な物価への上昇圧力となるとみられる。 
併せて、ドル円レートは足元で150 円/ドル台後半であり、前年同期比では円安水準にあ
る。原油価格の上昇も相まって輸入物価への押し上げ圧力が強い状態であり、消費者物価への
波及が見込まれる。 
また、賃上げによる人件費の増加分を販売価格に転嫁する動きも継続するだろう。26 年度春
闘における定期昇給（定昇）相当込みの賃上げ率は、加重平均で 5.01％と高水準を維持してい
る6。さらに、26 年度の最低賃金の全国加重平均額も前年度比 5％程度引き上げられる7。こう
した賃金上昇は、サービス価格を中心に物価を押し上げると見込んでいる。 
これらの物価上昇圧力は27 年度にかけても継続するだろう。他方、政府は飲食料品にかか
る消費税率を27 年4 月から2 年間1％に引き下げる方針である。引き下げが実施されれば、27
年度のコアCPI 上昇率は1％pt 超押し下げられ、前年比＋1％台まで縮小するだろう。

Document 060: 2026年9月财政数据快评：收入向下，力度向上

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年9月财政数据快评：收入向下，力度向上.pdf
Report source: 国信证券
Date: 2026-10-25
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

事项：
前三季度，全国一般公共预算收入约 17.2万亿元，同比增长 5.1%。其中，全国税收收入约 14.1万亿元，
同比增长 6.1%。全国一般公共预算支出约 21.0万亿元，同比增长 1.0%。
评论：
9 月财政收入增速下滑，但政策力度指数继续回升。
第一本账收入转负，支出基本持平。9月收入同比下降 0.1%，较前值（4.7%）回落转负；税收收入中增值
税、个人所得税为主要拉动，消费税仍为最大单项拖累，企业所得税、证券交易印花税转负。支出当月同
比 0.03%，基本持平。后续随着基数抬高，收入将延续回落，支出端则预计逐渐向着预算增速追赶。
第二本账收入降幅再度扩大，支出降幅收窄。9 月第二本账收入同比-15.9%，土地出让收入同比-23.2%，
降幅均再度扩大，后续随着地产新政落地可能难有较大回升；支出同比下降 12.3%，降幅明显收窄，主要
是土地以外的其他支出降幅明显收窄，反映债务资金拨付有所加快。
总体来看，广义财政收支增速开始相向而行，力度指数连续回升。9月广义收入当月同比转负、广义支出

降幅明显收窄，二者增速差显著收窄，广义财政缺口同比收缩幅度也有所放缓。四季度只要增速达到 4.2%
以上，全年经济增速就能完成 4.5%的目标，5500 亿债务限额下达后，再出台增量政策的必要性并不算太
强。
风险提示：海外市场动荡，存在不确定性。
 一般公共预算：收入转负、支出持平
2026年10月24日

### Page 2

9 月财政收入小幅转负。1-9 月财政收入累计同比 5.1%，前值（1-8月）5.7%，有所回落。其中税收收入
累计 6.1%，前值 6.6%。单月来看，一般公共预算收入当月同比-0.1%，前值 4.7%，小幅转负。税收收入当
月同比 0.2%，前值 5.6%，连续第二个月回落；非税收入当月同比-1.0%，前值 0.3%，再度转负。税收显著
回落，主要是去年基数继续抬高叠加企业所得税等主体税种走弱；非税收入短暂转正后再度小幅负增长，
收入端整体进入趋势性回落通道，预计后续可能会进一步回落，逐渐靠近年初预算的收入目标（2.2%）。
图1：一般公共预算收入累计与当月增速 图2：一般公共收入月度进度
资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理
图3：税收收入与非税收入增速 图4：各税种同比增速与前值对比
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
主要税种增速分化，增值税回升、消费税仍为最大拖累。主要税种中增值税同比 7.3%，较前值（4.0%）回升，

拉动税收收入 3.1个百分点、为最大拉动项；个人所得税同比 10.5%，较前值（11.8%）小幅回落，拉动税
收 1.1个百分点，累计同比 14.1%，仍在高速增长区间；企业所得税同比-0.4%，较前值（11.6%）明显回
落转负；消费税同比-8.5%，降幅较前值（-14.0%）收窄，拖累税收 1.0个百分点、仍为最大的单项拖累。
房地产相关税种整体降幅由-1.9%明显扩大至-14.6%，其中契税（-16.9%）仍然疲弱，土地增值税（-22.2%）
再度转负。此外，证券交易印花税同比-11.5%（前值 17.9%），在 2025 年 9 月同比 342%的超高基数上转
负，拖累税收 0.3 个百分点，后续可能随成交量走弱持续降低；进口环节增值税和消费税同比 12.9%，拉
动税收 1.9个百分点，与增速较高的进口数据较为一致。

### Page 3

图5：各税种对税收收入的拉动排序 图6：主要税种同比增速
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
支出端与上月基本持平。一般公共支出当月同比 0.03%，较前值（-0.1%）小幅回升。节奏上，一般公共预
算支出当月完成 9.6%，与去年同期（9.7%）基本一致。累计来看，1-9月第一本账支出增速为 1.0%，累计
进度 70.0%，与去年同期（70.1%）大体相当；但离 4.4%的预算支出增速水平差距仍然较大，后续有待存
量资金加快拨付带动支出增速回升，全年增速预计回升到 2-3%之间。
结构上，民生支出转负、基建降幅收窄。民生类支出同比增速-0.9%，较前值（1.9%）回落转负。其中，
教育支出同比增长 3.5%，前值 1.3%；社保就业支出-4.8%，前值 2.6%；卫生健康支出同比-2.9%，较前值
（3.8%）回落；文化娱乐体育传媒支出增速 1.6%，由负转正（前值-9.6%）。基建类支出同比增速-1.3%，
前值-3.7%，拖累支出 0.3个百分点。其中交通运输支出同比-10.8%，前值-5.8%，降幅扩大；城乡社区事

务支出增速由前值-3.8%转正至 7.9%；农林水事务支出同比-0.2%，较前值（1.1%）小幅回落；环保支出-13.7%，
降幅继续收窄（前值-15.8%）。此外，债务付息支出同比-1.2%，较前值（7.0%）转负；科学技术支出同
比 6.9%，较前值（9.0%）回落，仍维持较快增长。

 政府性基金预算：收入降幅再度扩大、支出降幅收窄
9 月政府性基金收入同比-15.9%（前值-3.8%），降幅再度扩大。土地出让收入（-23.2%）拖累重新加重，
同样较前值（-12.6%）明显扩大。政府性基金支出同比-12.3%，降幅较前值（-21.4%）明显收窄，主要是
除土地出让收入相关支出外的其他支出降幅由-18.0%收窄至-8.4%，反映专项债、特别国债资金拨付有所
加快。
1-9月，第二本账收入累计增速为-18.6%，支出增速为-16.2%。其中土地出让收入 16087 亿，同比-27.9%，
降幅较 1-8月（-28.6%）收窄，但仍处深度负增长区间，按当前降幅外推，全年仍不及 3 万亿。后续来看，

随着专项债、超长期特别国债资金拨付加快与新型政策性金融工具持续投放，第二本账支出端有望延续修
复。

### Page 5

图13：政府性基金收支增速 图14：土地使用权出让收入与支出增速
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
 总结：财政力度指数继续回升
财政力度指数连续第二个月回升。一方面是收入转负的同时支出降幅收窄。9月广义支出增速-3.7%，较前
值-6.7%明显收窄；广义收入当月同比增速-3.5%，前值 2.9%，由正转负。1-9月，广义财政支出累计增速
为-3.5%，完成度 65.2%，低于去年同期（67.1%）；广义财政收入累计增速为 1.4%，完成度 70.8%。另一
方面是赤字端同比收缩幅度放缓。1-9 月累计的广义财政缺口为 7.6万亿，较 2025年同期（8.8万亿）减
少约 1.3 万亿，同比收缩 14.4%，收缩幅度较 1-8 月（17.7%）有所收窄。此外，9 月新增财政存款-4300
亿，季末支出加快带动财政存款季节性回落，月末财政存款余额 7.9万亿；当月社融口径政府债券净融资
1.54万亿，较前值（1.01 万亿）明显回升。9月财政力度指数回升，连续第二个月回升。

后续来看，财政收支的 K 型分化将继续收敛。9月广义财政收入与支出增速已开始相向而行，政府债净融
资明显回升，8000 亿新型政策性金融工具持续投放，四季度专项债和超长期特别国债剩余额度发行使用仍
在途中。预计广义财政支出增速有望继续回升，关注政府债资金使用节奏与实物工作量形成情况。此外，
四季度只要增速达到 4.2%以上，全年经济增速就能完成 4.5%的目标，出台增量政策的必要性并不算太强，
现在的问题并非缺钱而是落地端。
图15：广义财政支出同比增速 图16：财政力度指数拆解

Document 066: 2026金融街论坛年会：完善稳定机制，深化投融资改革

### Page 1

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

完善稳定机制，深化投融资改革——2026 金融
街论坛年会 
投资观点：

宏观 
报告日期 2026-10-22 专题报告 
  ⚫ 央行完善流动性支持。拟设非银机构大额赎回流动性支持
工具，扩大 SLF 适用范围，并推进利率框架改革，改善市
场稳定基础和利率传导。 
⚫ 证监会推进投融资改革。通过主动 ETF、长周期考核、公
募基金改革和回购增持改善投资条件，同时推进发行上
市、并购重组和再融资改革，支持企业发展。 
⚫ 风险治理与跨境便利化同步推进。金融监管总局加强机构
风险处置和科技金融服务，外汇局拟推出跨境结算、投融
资和汇率风险管理便利化措施。 
⚫ 股指持续修复仍需资金与盈利配合 。政策有助于稳定预
期、改善配置条件，后续重点关注权益资金持续流入，以
及盈利改善向更多行业和成分股扩散。

### Page 2

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

一、2026金融街论坛年会 
2026 金融街论坛年会于 10 月 19 日在北京金融街开幕，会期为 10 月 19 日至 22 日，由北
京市人民政府与中国人民银行、国家金融监督管理总局、中国证监会、新华通讯社、国家外
汇管理局共同主办，主题为“开放·合作·包容——共筑全球金融发展新生态” 。 
本届年会采用“主论坛 +平行论坛+专题大会+配套活动”架构，包含主论坛、平行论坛
及三场专题大会：金融科技大会聚焦智能经济；保险大会首次由金融监管总局主办，聚焦保
险业高质量发展，并拟发布相关研究成果；投资大会聚焦跨境投资与资本对接。年会首次将
分会场拓展至非洲，在肯尼亚内罗毕设置分会场，并安排北京、香港、伦敦三地连线。四部
门主要负责人出席开幕式并释放重要政策信号。论坛兼具政策发布、行业交流、国际合作和
投融资对接功能。 
二、会议解读 
本次论坛上，央行、金融监管总局、证监会、外汇局负责人先后发言，政策信号主要体
现为：完善金融市场稳定机制，同时改善资金参与资本市场的制度条件。前者重在防止流动
性冲击被放大，后者重在提高资金配置的稳定性和效率，两者有利于改善市场运行基础。 
1、央行行长潘功胜：完善流动性支持，提高利率传导效率 
央行提出设立应对非银机构大额赎回的流动性支持工具。当资管机构面临群体性流动性
压力时，产品集中赎回可能迫使机构出售债券，债券价格下跌又会压低产品净值，诱发更多
赎回，形成负反馈。新工具对此提供应对机制，旨在防止债券市场的流动性冲击扩大并向其
他市场传递。工具在特定压力情景下启用，操作须满足宏观审慎要求、提供高等级抵押品。
此外，扩大常备借贷便利（ SLF）适用范围，重在完善银行体系的临时流动性支持渠道；推
动银行间与交易所债券市场互联互通，有助于改善跨市场交易、流动性配置和价格形成。 
利率框架改革是另一条重要线索，涉及利率调控与贷款定价的“基准体系” 。具体包括三
个方面：一是研究择机将公开市场主要操作品种的期限由 7 天切换为隔夜，使公开市场隔夜
回购利率相应成为主要政策利率，提高利率调控的精准性；二是推动贷款定价基准多元化，
未来随着相关机制完善，财务能力强的企业可与金融机构协商，自主选择 DR001 或 LPR 作为

### Page 3

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

浮动利率贷款定价基准，逐步拓宽 LPR 以外的定价选择；三是推动原存贷款基准利率退出。
该利率自 2015 年 10 月以来长期未作调整，目前在存贷款定价中已不再发挥作用，下一步将
推动司法等少数领域的计息依据改用市场化利率指标。 
2、证监会主席吴清：完善稳市机制，推进投融资改革和市场治理 
吴清的讲话围绕市场稳定、包容性改革、市场生态和对外开放展开。投资端重点是健全
长周期考核机制、拓宽中长期资金入市渠道、深化公募基金改革，并宣布证监会当日将同意
首批 18 只主动 ETF 注册。同时，鼓励有条件的上市公司加大回购增持力度。这些安排分别作
用于机构考核、产品供给和股东回报，有助于提高权益配置的稳定性。 
融资端重点是落实“两创板”改革，推进发行上市、并购重组和再融资制度调整，提高
资本市场对科技企业和不同发展阶段企业的适应性。同时，支持证券公司并购重组及后续整
合，提升投资机构服务能力；推进北证 50ETF、北交所新股市值配售等安排，完善服务创新
型中小企业的市场体系。 
市场治理方面，筹建基金业运行监测中心、规范人工智能在证券期货业的应用、完善违
规减持处罚规则和先行赔付制度，分别加强风险监测、技术治理、交易约束和投资者保护。
政策着重提高监管有效性和市场规则的可预期性，为长期投资提供制度保障。 
对外开放方面，提升 QFII 投资便利度，支持境内外机构开发更多中国资产相关产品，推
进人民币股票交易柜台和 REITs 纳入沪深港通，拓宽跨境投资渠道。同时，强调防范跨境跨
市场风险传导，关注境外挂钩 A 股个股的杠杆产品等新风险点。 
3、金融监管总局局长丁向群、外汇局局长朱鹤新：推进风险治理、
机构转型和跨境便利化 
金融监管总局强调在风险治理基础上推进机构转型和资源配置调整。丁向群提出，通过
兼并重组、市场退出等方式稳妥处置高风险机构，强化早期纠正和穿透监管，减少风险积累
及其传染。同时，在保障基础服务、考虑承载能力和保持适度竞争的条件下，因地制宜推进
中小机构减量提质，推动机构形成与自身能力相匹配的业务定位。 
金融服务结构调整同样重要。银行资金要更好适应先进制造业、服务业和基础设施建设
需要，保险则要增强灾害、航运、养老健康和科技创新等领域的保障能力。支持商业航天、

### Page 4

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

生物医药、智能机器人等领域专业共保体建设，说明科技金融不仅涉及提供融资，也涉及分
担研发和产业化风险。相关业务能否形成稳定盈利，取决于金融机构的专业服务、风险定价
和资本管理能力。 
外汇局重在提高企业跨境经营便利度，完善双向资金配置条件。朱鹤新介绍，拟推出的
增量措施涉及经常项目便利化、资本项目外汇管理优化，以及外汇市场开放和证券公司结售
汇业务试点。政策直接服务于企业跨境结算、投融资和汇率风险管理，重在降低业务办理成
本，提高资金配置效率。 
这一方向同时服务“引进来”和“走出去” 。结合央行关于人民币清算、离岸市场和流动
性供给的部署，相关政策有助于改善企业跨境经营和人民币资产配置的基础条件。对 A 股的
影响主要通过经营成本、融资条件和投资便利度传导。外资是否增加权益配置，仍取决于盈
利、估值、汇率和全球资产的相对吸引力。 
3、股指启发 
本届论坛的政策意义，主要在于稳定市场预期、改善长期投资条件，并支持企业融资与
经营。稳市机制和流动性支持，有助于减少集中赎回、被动抛售和跨市场风险传染，改善投
资者持有权益资产的信心；主动 ETF、投资便利化和长周期考核，有助于丰富配置渠道、减
少短期业绩压力对投资行为的干扰，回购增持则通过实际执行形成购买需求。这些政策对股
指的支持，需逐步落实为持续配置和盈利改善。 
对股指而言，长期投资条件改善，有利于盈利稳定、股东回报较好的企业获得配置支持 。
融资改革和科技金融则为相关企业研发、产业化和盈利增长提供条件。政策效果将通过资金
配置与企业经营逐步体现 。若盈利改善扩散至更多行业和成分股，并伴随持续资金流入，指
数修复基础将更稳固。若经营兑现偏慢或风险偏好回落，市场仍可能反复。

### Page 5

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

和宏观研究。曾获期货日报"最佳宏观金融期货分析师"称号。

股指与金融衍生品研究，擅长将机器学习、人工智能等量化技术应用于股指及金融衍生品研
究领域，构建套利、趋势跟踪等交易策略。

thomaschang-72182@esunsec.com.tw

法 說 前 瞻              產業：電子零組件 
國巨法說會前財報預估 
預估 4Q26 營運逐步墊高，仍關注未來獲利狀況

| 財報訊息   
國巨即將於 10/29(四)舉行法說會， 3Q26營收季增 11.4%至495.3億
元，符合預期，市場普遍預估毛利率介於38~40%，EPS約4.5元，後
續將關注公司對於2027年產能規劃 、 整體產業供需(同業擴廠狀況及
AI機櫃時程)以及產品價格看法。 
| 營運現況  
3Q26營收符合預期：3Q26營收季增11.4%至495.3億元，主要受惠於
AI相關應用需求，且標準品及特殊品出貨均呈現穩定成長，營收達
成率100%，符合我們先前預期，而獲利部分，隨3Q26將有新合約逐
漸發酵，推測獲利將較上季提升，毛利率和營益率我們預估兩者分
別為39.5%和28.8%，稅後EPS達5.4元。 
| 營運展望  
本季營收預估季增 5%至520億元：我們預估4Q26營收有望季增5%
至520億元 ， 成長動能來自於AI新平台陸續放亮對於元件的需求 、 日
韓大廠的外溢訂單以及產品組合轉佳，且從10月代理商提供的交期
數據觀察，村田及三星電機的MLCC交期已長達約30及52週，趨勢
持續往上，若對照疫情期間的高峰(3Q21村田高容MLCC曾達30~33
週)，顯示供需仍吃緊，而目前擴廠及設備準備時間較長，各廠僅藉
由產線優化及產線轉換提升約10%~15%，因此推測B/B ratio 也保持
在高檔，獲利部分，根據供應鏈調查，部分日韓廠有漲價動作，國
巨有望跟進漲價，因此預估毛利率將延續上升趨勢，提升 0.75個百
分點至40.3%，稅後EPS達5.75元。

 投資評價與建議 
維持買進：(1) 美洲持續暢旺到明年春節，亞洲進入旺季，運價淡季相對有撐，(2)

歐洲線艙位成長 7.5%，貨量成長 12.2%。地緣政治風險導致供應鏈不穩旺季提前
運送而上漲， 亞洲重要港口碼頭使用率偏高， 天候因素容易導致碼頭作業效率低造
成塞港與巴拿馬運河水位降低等均影響有效運力，支撐美東線運價重回 1 萬美元
/FEU 關卡，10/8 巴拿馬運河水位 85.2 英尺(-1.93%YoY)較 9 月中略有回升，但
11 月底水位雖回升到 85.9 英尺但低於去年同期 3.4%，航商預期美洲線貨量持續
暢旺到農曆春節，觀察 2Q27 美國是否啟動升息循環影響貨量。

2026 年供過增長率最低的一年：Alphaliner 預估 2026 需求成長 2.5%， 供給成長
率 4.7%，2026 年船舶供給成長率是近幾年最低的，目前貨櫃船在手訂單1400 萬
TEU，未來三年面臨新船大量下水壓力，然而公司認為受到地緣政治影響船期不穩
定性高、碼頭使用率偏高易受突發事件導致塞港、2028 年美西港口工人合約到期
等均影響有效運力，即使紅海復航採逐班逐航線，影響也會被抵銷，因此2027 年
的名目供給能否 100%轉為實質運力有待考驗 。 目前全球運力因為碼頭作業能量受
限、氣候因素、繞道、降速等影響 11%運力。

燃油成本的影響 ：新加坡船用燃油 3Q 月平均 574 美元/噸(-7%QoQ%+44%YoY)，
低硫油價格 3Q 月平均 798 美元/噸(+3%QoQ%,+58%YoY)，長榮安裝脫硫塔的
船舶超過 9 成，船齡相對年輕省油，2025 年燃油成本佔 21%，1H26 佔 22%，
2Q 反應油價上漲會提升。長榮 2025 年平均用油價格 485 美元/噸(-8.1%YoY)，
1-8M26 用油價格 581 美元/噸(+16.7%YoY)，1-8 月燃油成本提升到 23%。 
現金殖利率 ：預估明後年配發現金股利 20 元和 18 元 ， 現金殖利率為8.6%和 7.7%，
長線受高股息 ETF 的青睞。 
預估 3Q26 稅後獲利 413 億元(+90%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 19.08 元：3Q 旺季運價
亮眼 ， 營收1468 億元(+51%YoY)符合預期 ， 預估稅後獲利413 億元(+90%YoY)，
稅後 EPS 為 19.08 元。公司目前北美訂艙量到 11 月都相當樂觀，4Q 預估營收
1320 億元(+54%YoY)，稅後獲利 264 億元(+210%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 12.20 元。 
財務預估 ： 預估 2026 年營收 4704 億元 (+24%YoY)，稅後獲利 921 億元
(+34%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 42.52 元，每股淨值 290.4 元。2027 年雖然紅海逐漸
復航，但碼頭作業彈性低易塞港抵銷運力恢復，預估營收4666 億元(-0.8%YoY)，
稅後獲利 792 億元(-14%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 36.56 元，每股淨值 326.9 元。

### Page 1

M
Update
Macronix International Co Ltd  | Asia Pacific
Pricing hike lifts margin further;
shifting from NOR toward
NAND
3Q earnings affected by capacity reallocation:
• 3Q revenue: NT$24.2bn, +27% Q/Q, +195% Y/Y, 9% below our estimate; we
cite to capacity transition from NOR to NAND.
• Gross margin: 70.5%, +6.1ppt Q/Q, +57.0ppt Y/Y, 2.7ppt above our estimate –
helped by ~NT$750mn inventory reversal, mostly associated with NOR..
• EPS: NT$5.52, +41% Q/Q, 13% below our estimate.

•  Employee compensation accruals led to a 24% Q/Q operating expense
increase.
Shifting focus to NAND: NOR revenue increased 21% Q/Q despite a 12% Q/Q
decline in bit shipments, consistent with pricing strength. Management attributed
lower NOR shipments to capacity reallocation toward NAND; NAND revenue rose
39% Q/Q, mainly from pricing.
The first phase of capacity expansion is scheduled to enter volume production in
2H27; the additional NT$15.8bn investment could contribute in 2028.

Incremental capacity will favor NAND, in particular 3D NAND-based eMMC (i.e., MLC
NAND), with no planned increase in NOR capacity. The company will gradually
withdraw from ROM product.

### Page 3

Update
Morgan Stanley Research 3
Valuation Methodology and Risks
Macronix International Co Ltd (2337.TW)
Base case, P/B, in line with our Greater China memory coverage, due to the industry's cyclical
nature. We apply a target multiple of 2.1x to our 2027 BVPS estimate vs. its historical average
of 1.5x since 2017. We continue to believe the P/B target is justifiable, as the MLC and legacy
TLC NAND could be in greater shortage into 2H26, with under-supply easily reaching 40%. We

believe the only supplier that could fill the gap remains Macronix.
Risks to Upside
n Wider legacy NAND supply/demand gap
n NOR up-cycle, driven by limited supply expansion and/or stronger demand
n Faster growth for Switch hardware and core titles
Risks to Downside
n Less severe legacy NAND supply/demand gap
n NOR down-cycle, driven by faster supply expansion and/or demand weakness
n Slower growth for Switch hardware and core titles

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.

營收走勢圖

研究員/莊佳蓉 EVA8894122@uni-psg.com

研究部對緯穎(6669)維持買進建議，主係：(1) 3Q26營收季增56%，
大幅優於預期；(2) 4Q26在AWS的ASIC氣冷出貨帶動下，預期4Q營
收季增個位數 ；(3) 雖T3水冷機櫃短期遞延， 但預估27年逐月攀升，
公司仍為主要供應商。(4) AMD MI450 於1H27年放量，客戶與平台
更加多元；(5) 新增之Oracle VR200預期的訂單約2.5K，約在27年
貢獻營收。 綜上所述，研究部上修 2026/2027 年營收至1.46兆元
/3.07兆元(原估1.27兆元/2.38兆元)，EPS上修至143.16元/241.16
元，目前評價僅8~9倍，位於低檔，給予12.5倍本益比，目標價3010
元(2027EPS*12.5PER)。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26營收大幅優於預期 
緯穎9月營收為1,726億元，月增19.6%，年增100%，整體3Q26
營收達4,346億元 ，季增56.2%，年增63%，營收達成率達到
135%(研究部預估)/128%(市場共識)， 大幅優於預期 ， 主係Meta
通用型及AWS T3氣冷出貨旺盛所致。3Q26財報尚未公布，研究
部上修至毛利率8.9%(原估8.1%)， 季減0.4pts， 優於原先預期 ，
主係通用伺服器占比較高， 產品組合較佳 ，對毛利率形成支撐，
預估3Q EPS約45.56元。

2. 4Q營運動能可望延續 
展望4Q26，雖水冷機櫃須遞延至年底至1H27才會逐漸放量 ，但
仍預期T3氣冷機櫃及通用持續出貨，預估4Q26營收約4,666億
元，季增7.4%，年增59.5%。毛利率8.2% (原估7.3%)，季減
0.7pts， 主係高單價ASIC水冷機櫃占比尚低， 預估EPS約45.18
元。整體而言，2H26在ASIC氣冷及通用伺服器帶動下，營運明
顯優於上半年

3. T3短期雜音不改長期趨勢，2027年水冷逐步放量 
根據供應鏈調查：(1) T3水冷機櫃初期產線磨合不順，客戶因
此下修今年出貨規劃。不過，2027年水冷機櫃出貨規劃仍逐季
攀升，在晶片端的水冷比重也提升 至50~70%，單價為氣冷3-4
倍以上，成為2027年營收成長的主要動能。在AWS下一代T4產
品，公司持續參與機櫃設計，有望採用全水冷設計，預期28年
出貨，為中長期成長動能。

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Central banks’ communication is carefully scrutinised for
every nuance. Lately, there seems to have been more RBA
communication to pore over. The method of delivery has
also evolved. This has sometimes caused confusion when
tone seems to vary. Different audiences call for different

tone and varying complexity.
• The content has evolved, too. RBA officials are increasingly
using speeches directed to professional audiences to
discuss more technical estimates of inferred variables such
as neutral rates and financial conditions. They are also
highlighting that monetary policy combats inflation in more
ways than just squeezing mortgage holders.
• One thing has not changed: the RBA is not in the business
of providing ‘forward guidance’ promises about the future

path of interest rates. It does, however, give more running
commentary about how it is interpreting recent data. These
interpretations are themselves useful signals about what
the RBA thinks it might do.
Market participants pore over every utterance by central banks
for clues to future rate moves. They will have plenty to pore over
in the run-up to the November RBA decision. After the flurry
of fireside chats this week, ’60 Minutes’ will run a pre-recorded

interview with chief economist Sarah Hunter on Sunday. Next
Wednesday, the whole senior policy team will appear before
Senate Estimates, just as the quarterly CPI is released. (This
timing will have been the Senate’s choice, not the RBA’s.) After
the decision will come the Statement on Monetary Policy, a
media release and press conference. More speeches will follow,
including one by the Deputy Governor on the Thursday.
RBA communication does seem to have changed, not just

following the RBA Review but also more recently. There may
be fewer policy meetings each year, but the communication
outside that certainly feels more frequent than a couple of years
ago. Back in 2024, speaking engagements felt a little sparse
outside the post-meeting media conference and parliamentary
appearances. We have counted 58 public engagements so
far this year on the Speeches page on the RBA’s website.
This compares with 58 for the whole of last year and 52 in

2024. Excluding the ‘compulsory’ press conferences and
parliamentary appearances, fewer than half of the 2024 events
were related to economics, markets and monetary policy.
The ‘how’ has changed
The way the RBA communicates with markets has changed
as well. There are fewer set-piece speeches on policy and a
Open mouth operations
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
lot more semi-scripted ‘fireside chats’. Compared with a few
years ago, there are more interviews across a wide range of

media. This is a big change from the pre-Review world. It was
a huge deal when the previous Governor appeared on the
7:30 Report ; it is hard to imagine him agreeing to an Assistant
Governor appearing on 60 Minutes.
Part of the change reflects increased efforts to reach a general
audience. Central banks want to explain why they do what
they do. This matters especially when interest rates are rising
but the household sector is not the source of the inflation.

Speaking differently to different audiences can sometimes
confuse markets. As the RBA’s Chief Communication Officer
highlighted in a recent panel discussion, the post-meeting
media conference is designed to speak to the public. Market
participants and economists certainly tune in, but we are not
the primary audience. So, if you think that what the Governor
said in the media conference sounded more ‘dovish’ than
what another official says in a more market-oriented forum,

it is partly because she is trying to convey that the RBA
understands that its recent policy actions are making things
harder for many Australians. But this is in much the same way
that a doctor might say, “This might hurt a bit.” It does, but that
will not stop the treatment.
A related issue is that it is easier to control tone in pre-drafted
material such as the minutes and set-piece speeches than in
unstructured Q&A. Senior central bankers are trained to be

careful with their words in unscripted settings. If the tone
differs, the minutes are still more likely to say what the Board
really means than an off-the-cuff answer.
Greater communication with the broader public might have
reduced the pressure to make the set-piece speeches more
accessible. Research on central bank communication often
uses measures such as the Flesch–Kincaid Grade Level and
Flesch Reading Ease score. A comprehensive analysis was

beyond the scope of this note. However, a spot comparison
suggests four speeches from 2026 ( 1, 2, 3, 4) were harder
to read than four from 2021 (1, 2, 3, 4). The 2021 speeches
scored in the 11 to 12 range, which is high-school-level
“The RBA does not want to promise what it will
do, but it is increasingly trying to tell us what
it thinks. "

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
complexity. The 2026 speeches scored between 12 and
15, which is academic or technically dense text requiring
university-level education. (Including two of my own 2021
speeches is an obvious caveat, but the two speeches by then-
Governor Phil Lowe also scored better on these metrics than

the sample from this year. It should be noted, though, that
readability metrics do not necessarily imply better clarity if the
concepts discussed are more abstract.)
The ‘what’ has changed
As noted above, the content of RBA communication has
evolved, too. As its intellectual framework has begun to put
more weight on the balance of supply and demand, and those
invisible ‘star’ variables (NAIRUs, neutral real rates, ‘financial
conditions’, etc.), the communication to professional audiences

has increasingly focused on those topics.
More recently, the RBA has also placed more emphasis on
the full range of transmission channels from interest rates to
inflation (though not, as we have noted previously, the labour
supply channel). This is understandable in a world of supply
shocks. People need to understand that monetary policy is not
simply an exercise in hitting mortgage borrowers. We doubt
they would be comforted, though, by the Deputy Governor’s

explanation this week that the ‘cashflow channel’ was the
smallest of the transmission channels from interest rates
to inflation, with the exchange rate much more prominent.
That implies a lot of pain for a small group, for little effect on
inflation. It raises the question of whether other policy tools
or institutional arrangements might get the same effect on
inflation through the other channels without hitting a minority
of households disproportionately.

We have also seen a move away from using catchphrases
to summarise the position. From ‘narrow path’ to ‘not
ruling anything in or out’ to ‘cautious and predictable’, once
entrenched, a phrase can outlast its usefulness.
One thing that has not changed is the way the minutes record
the presentations to the Board and the members’ discussion. The
minutes rarely convey much new information beyond the post-
meeting statements. Sometimes, though, the minutes reveal the

Board’s reaction to the staff’s analysis. The August 2024 minutes
were a good example: the discussion highlighted just how
sensitive the forecasts were to key judgements by the staff.
The ‘why’ has not changed
Despite these changes, the underlying objectives of the RBA’s
communication have not changed. It wants to explain – and
perhaps justify – its decisions. And it wants to describe how
it interprets incoming data, especially inflation, the labour

market and the balance of supply and demand.
The RBA, like any central bank, is not really in the business
of providing ‘forward guidance’ about future moves.
Circumstances change, so it can never guarantee a particular
future path for interest rates. Some academic literature argues
that promising to keep rates low for a long time (or high,
for that matter) independently amplifies the transmission
of monetary policy beyond the effect of current-period

interest rates alone, by shifting the short end of the yield
curve. Central banks including the RBA have certainly tried
jawboning in this way from time to time. The prevailing view,
though, is that the potential cost of reputational loss if things
do not turn out as predicted outweighs the benefits of a
slightly stronger transmission mechanism.
In this context, it is the interpretation of data that provides
the signal about future policy directions. This is why it was

noteworthy that the Governor still characterised the labour
market as ‘a little tight’ after the September meeting, even
though the Board’s post-meeting communication did not. We
have been noting for some time that the RBA staff have a
downbeat view of supply capacity. Together with the RBA’s
oft-stated concerns about inflation, this is why the Deputy
Governor’s ‘hawkishness’ did not surprise us.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: managing expectations
While annual retail sales growth improved in September,
rising from 0.4%yr to 1.1%yr, the current pace of growth
is only a quarter of the gain seen just a year ago and very
weak versus history. Consumer spending continues to be

constrained by weak confidence, particularly with respect to
employment prospects and wealth. On housing wealth, five
years into the property downturn, house prices continue to
decline month-on-month and investment remains in a deep
downturn, –20.6%ytd.
Total fixed asset investment has been detracting from
annual GDP growth since April owing to a lull in high-tech
investment, a lack of confidence in future demand amongst
domestically oriented businesses as well as property’s

malaise. For aggregate growth to hold up through 2027 and
2028 as the contribution from net exports moderates, broad-
based stimulus in scale will prove necessary. Expect recent
targeted support measures to be broadened and given much
greater scale in coming months.
Finally, in the UK, inflation accelerated from 3.1%yr to 3.5%yr
in September, exceeding both market expectations and the
Bank of England's July forecast. The surprise was largely
driven by energy, fuel prices rising 5.4%mth. However, core

inflation also edged up from 2.6%yr to 2.8%yr as services
inflation increased from 3.4%yr to 3.7%yr. While not an
immediate concern inflation, the labour market showing
resilience, the ILO unemployment rate falling from 4.9% to
4.7% in August, suggests demand risks for inflation will need
close monitoring as well as supply-side concerns.
Next week will see the focus once again return to the US as
the FOMC meet and US Q3 GDP is released. For both events,

the implications for risks to the outlook will be the focus. The
week after, the US mid-term elections are due.
With little domestic data of significance, participants’
attention focused on RBA Deputy Governor Hauser's fireside
chat, which saw an abrupt shift in market expectations of a
follow-up rate hike at the November meeting.
Hauser warned in his remarks that the current inflation outlook
was "materially worse" than the RBA's August forecasts.

Three upside inflation risks were characterised as having
"crystallised" ahead of the September meeting – the Middle
East, the AI boom, and domestic supply constraints. Hauser
noted the Middle East picture "looks worse" given oil prices
are "about a third higher than [the RBA] assumed in August”.
He then positioned AI and data centre developments among
an "extraordinary sequence of positive demand shocks around
the world", with "higher AI prices, tech prices feeding through

into CPI". Finally, on domestic capacity pressures, the Deputy
Governor noted that "inflation's been coming in at or a little
bit above expectations, despite an apparent easing in the
labour market". He went on to state that the staff are currently
assessing "whether [the RBA] need to change [its] judgement
on capacity pressures in the labour market again" – in other
words, revise up their assumption for the NAIRU.
Hauser's comments prompted a material reassessment of

market pricing for a follow-up November hike, from just a
20% chance at the end of last week to a 50/50 proposition
today ahead of next week's Q3 CPI. For that release, we are
forecasting a 1.3% (4.1%yr) lift in headline inflation and a
0.9 (3.5%yr) increase in trimmed mean inflation. Our preview
provides more detail behind the component mix for Q3 as
well as our upward revisions to the preliminary Q4 forecast.
Provided inflation prints broadly in line with our expectations,

the RBA is likely to hike by 25bp at the November meeting.
Across in New Zealand, consumer prices rose 0.7% in Q3
and annual inflation eased from 4.1%yr to 3.7%yr. Under
the surface of this ‘as expected’ read though, several closely
watched measures of core inflation took a step higher to now
be at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s target band. Our
NZ economics team consequently expect the RBNZ will raise
the OCR 25bps to 3.00% at its October policy review, while

retaining a 4.00% terminal OCR forecast for September 2027.
Earlier in the week, China's economy surprised  to the upside in
Q3, GDP growth accelerating from 0.9%qtr to 1.2%qtr, lifting
annual growth from 4.3%yr to 4.5%yr – keeping authorities on
track to achieve their 4.5–5.0% full-year growth objective.
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist
“With little domestic data of significance,

Governor Hauser's fireside chat. Offshore,
China GDP was the key release. "

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
RBNZ now expected to hike in October
We now expect the RBNZ will deliver a 25bp hike at its
policy review on 28 October. While September quarter
inflation was slightly below the RBNZ’s forecast, the
underlying detail was firm. More recently, increases in

fuel prices point to a material lift in inflation by year-end.
We expect further interest rate increases over the coming
months, with the OCR reaching a peak of 4% next year.
Softer headline inflation but firm core pressures
leave RBNZ on course to hike in October
We now expect the RBNZ to raise the Official Cash Rate
by 25bp at its October policy review (previously, we had
expected a pause at this meeting). This change in view reflects
significant upside risks to near-term inflation relative to the

RBNZ’s previous forecast.
While in line with our forecast, the September quarter inflation
figures were slightly softer than the RBNZ expected, with prices
rising 3.7% over the past year (compared with the RBNZ’s
forecast of 3.9%). However, the details were less reassuring.
Excluding volatile items such as food and fuel, underlying
inflation pressures remained firm. Closely watched measures
of core inflation moved higher and now sit close to, or a little

above, the top of the RBNZ’s target band. We also saw an
increase in the number of goods recording price rises, taking
that measure back to levels seen in the wake of the pandemic.
Alongside the existing firmness in underlying inflation, the
risks for inflation over the coming quarters are firmly tilted to
the upside. That near-term upside risk has also been noted by
the Governor. Following a sharp rise in global fuel costs, we
expect inflation to reach 4.3% by the end of this year (above

the RBNZ’s September MPS forecast for +3.9%). While that is
mainly due to higher prices at the pump, businesses in sectors
such as construction are also reporting related increases in
transport and materials costs. Consistent with those trends,
we expect core inflation to remain in the upper part of the
RBNZ’s target band through 2027.
Reinforcing the case for an OCR hike at the October meeting,
the New Zealand dollar is notably weaker than the RBNZ

assumed: on a trade-weighted basis, the NZD is currently
3.6% below the RBNZ’s September projections. If sustained,
that would add to inflation pressures over the next few
quarters. We suspect this will not have shifted the RBNZ’s
medium-term outlook for inflation.
Several other developments need to be weighed against that
firmer inflation outlook. First, wholesale long-term interest rates
have risen markedly, and there has also been a more moderate

rise in some mortgage rates. The RBNZ is likely to note the role of
those increases in managing medium-term inflation risks.
In addition, the RBNZ is likely to continue to emphasise that
there is little evidence of a lift in businesses’ longer-term plans
for price setting at this stage in response to the rise in energy
prices (sometimes referred to as “second-round inflation
effects”). We expect the RBNZ to highlight the risk that more
persistent inflation pressures could emerge, depending on the

duration of the oil price shock.
We also expect the RBNZ to note that the labour market
remains cyclically weak and is not a significant driver of inflation.
Employment growth that remains weak relative to labour force
growth, subdued growth in job vacancies and low labour market
turnover are consistent with this view and could be emphasised.
Finally, the RBNZ is likely to acknowledge that while the
global growth outlook remains solid, there are significant

downside risks from the Iran war and concerns about the
sustainability of the AI investment cycle.
Balancing those concerns, we think the MPC will lift the
cash rate next week. However, a vote seems very possible as
differences in view will likely remain among the MPC about
how much the medium-term inflation outlook has really
shifted in recent weeks.
In September, the bulk of the MPC saw upside risks to the
inflation outlook while two members (Conway and Hansen)

thought the risks were balanced. Recent developments will
not have made those seeing upside risks to inflation more
Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
comfortable, while the more dovish MPC members will
likely focus on the ongoing downside risks for growth given
continued softness in house prices and the mounting pressure
on real incomes. Indeed, all MPC members saw some degree
of downside risks to growth which likely remain relevant. All

these messages should remain in the RBNZ’s updated views.
The MPC is likely to continue to present its decision as a
gradual evolution of the approach taken since July: a higher
OCR now provides insurance against the need for a much
higher OCR later if upside risks to medium-term inflation
materialise. We doubt the aim will be to push market interest
rates materially higher.
RBNZ to leave door open for further hikes, OCR
still expected to peak at 4% in 2027

In its forward guidance, the RBNZ is likely to leave open the option
of raising the OCR further at the December Monetary Policy
Statement, without presenting an increase as certain. While the
RBNZ will not publish an updated OCR projection at this review,
the press statement is likely to note that further increases may
be needed, depending on economic conditions. The September
projection showed the OCR eventually rising to 3.25%.
We expect the RBNZ will end up delivering another 25bp

hike in December, followed by a further hike in February.
That would take the OCR to 3.5%, roughly where the RBNZ
estimates the neutral rate to be. At that point, we expect the
RBNZ to slow the pace of rate increases (previously, we had
expected that point to be reached a little later).
We continue to expect further 25bp hikes at the June and
September 2027 meetings, taking the OCR to a peak of 4.0%.
While oil prices are generating significant swings in inflation,

underlying pressures remain firm. More restrictive conditions are
likely to be needed to bring core inflation sustainably back to 2%.
Scenarios
Our central scenario, with a probability of around 60%, is that
the RBNZ increases the OCR by 25bp and signals that a further
increase in December remains an option, without committing to
a markedly tighter future stance than it outlined in September.
Alternative scenarios include:
• A hawkish scenario (around a 20% probability) would see

the OCR increase to 3%, with the RBNZ placing greater
emphasis on upside risks to core and medium-term inflation.
That would suggest an upward revision to the RBNZ’s
projected OCR path in December. Downside risks to the
exchange rate could also be emphasised.
• A dovish scenario (around a 20% probability) would
see the RBNZ leave the OCR unchanged and signal that
the medium-term inflation outlook has not changed
meaningfully from its September forecast, despite higher

near-term inflation. In this scenario, the RBNZ would
reiterate its previous forecast: a 25bp increase in December
and one further hike during 2027.
How might the election play into the decision?
While next week’s meeting is close to the General Election
on 7 November, the decision taken by the MPC will not be
politically motivated. However, the election outcome is close.
And should it result in a Labour Party-led coalition, it seems
likely the new government would adopt the Labour Party’s

promise to restore the RBNZ’s dual mandate. That would
require the MPC to weigh the degree of labour market slack
more highly in its deliberations. As the degree of labour market
slack currently seems high, a new mandate might justify the
MPC taking more time to return inflation to the middle of the
target range (our earlier note discusses this issue). This could
figure into the deliberations of some MPC members if the
decision between leaving the OCR unchanged and raising it

now is viewed as finely balanced.

AUS: Sep Private Sector Credit (%mth)
Oct 30, Last: 0.6, Westpac f/c: 0.5
Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.4 to 0.6
Private sector credit growth came in slightly firmer than
expected at 0.6%mth in August, above Westpac’s and the
market’s forecast for a 0.5%mth gain, leaving credit growth

running at 8.4%yr. Growth in housing credit continued to ease,
as we expected, though the mix highlights that the waning in
momentum is uneven, centred on investors rather than owner-
occupiers. Business credit meanwhile remains a source of
strength, notwithstanding some slowing recently.
For September, we expect credit growth to nudge to 0.5%mth,
with the divergence between housing credit and business
credit persisting.
AUS: Q3/Sep CPI (%qtr/%mth)

Oct 28, Quarter, Last: 0.6%, Westpac f/c: 1.3%,
Market f/c: 1.3%, Range: 0.6 to 1.4.
Oct 28, Month, Last: 0.4%, Westpac f/c: 0.6%,
Market f/c: 0.6 Range: 0.3 to 0.8
As noted in the review of the August CPI, our September
quarter CPI estimate is unchanged at 1.3%qtr holding the
annual pace flat at 3.9%yr. At the margin there was a 0.04ppt
increase due to a lift in auto fuel but it still rounds to 1.3%.
The seasonally adjusted CPI gains 1.1% highlighting that part

of the increase is due to usual seasonality.
In the month, the September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% with
the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Recreation & culture make
the largest contribution but transport is not far behind. Our
seasonally adjusted estimate increases 0.5%.
For more details please see “ September CPI Preview “.
AUS: Q3/Sep Trimmed Mean (%qtr/mth)
Oct 28, Quarter, Last: 0.8%, Westpac f/c: 0.9%,
Market f/c: 1.0%, Range: 0.9 to 1.0.

Oct 28, Month, Last: 0.2%, Westpac f/c: 0.3%,
Market f/c: 0.3%, Range: 0.2 to 0.4
Our September quarter Trimmed Mean estimate is unchanged
at 0.9%qtr with the annual pace dipping a touch from 3.6%yr
to 3.5%yr. There was a small downward revision of 0.03ppt to
0.88% compared to our original published estimate but it still
rounds to 0.9%qtr.
In the month of September we are looking for a 0.3% in
the Trimmed Mean holding the annual pace steady at 3.6%

which will be the fourth consecutive month at this pace. The
distribution of the September Trimmed Mean ranges from
–0.3% to 0.7%.
For more details please see “ September CPI Preview “.

NZ: RBNZ Official Cash Rate Review (%)
Oct 28, Last: 2.75, Westpac f/c: 3.00, Mkt f/c: 3.00
We expect the RBNZ to raise the OCR by 25bps to 3% at
the 28 October Review. Data since September show a higher
outlook for year-end inflation and a stronger GDP starting

point, but increased risks to domestic consumption and
residential investment. On balance, the medium-term inflation
picture will probably be judged to have worsened slightly
given recent movements in energy prices and the exchange
rate. A vote seems very possible as differences in view will
likely remain among the MPC about how much the medium-
term inflation outlook has really shifted in recent weeks.
Forward guidance will likely leave open the option to raise

the OCR further at the December Monetary Policy Statement
without presenting it as a sure thing.
NZ: Oct ANZ Business Confidence (index)
Oct 29, Last: 51.9
Firms remained optimistic in the September survey of business
confidence, with both the activity and inflation measures
broadly holding steady. However, it’s likely that most responses
were submitted in the early part of the month, while fuel prices
took a sharp step higher from the second half of September. The

survey comes too late for the RBNZ’s October policy review, but
evidence about the breadth of cost and price pressures could
have a bearing on the RBNZ’s thinking about the total amount of
policy tightening needed in the months ahead.
NZ: Sep Monthly Employment Indicator (%mth)
Oct 28, Last: 0.2, Westpac f/c: 0.3
The MEI has been gradually ticking higher in recent months,
showing that the jobs market has held its ground through
the Iran conflict and the consequent surge in fuel prices. The

weekly snapshots provided by Stats NZ have been running
ahead of year-ago levels and suggest another solid result for
September – we’ve pencilled in a 0.3% increase. Given the
history of this measure, however, both this month and the
previous month are likely to be revised down a little from their
initial release.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Oct FOMC meeting
Oct 27-28, Last: 3.875%, Mkt f/c: 3.875%, WBC f/c: 3.875%
Core CPI inflation and producer price inflation both surprised
to the upside in September, and the price of Brent oil has
risen back to around USD110. Underlying momentum in
the economy also remains robust, although employment

disappointed in the latest update. Given the concerns shown
by Committee members over price risks and their collective
resolve to return annual inflation to target, the above data
could easily justify a follow-up hike at the October meeting.
That said, Committee members who have spoken recently
have shown patience with respect to assessing the economy
and positioing policy. The market has responded, effectively
pricing out the chance of an October hike in favour of a

December move, which is also our expectation.
US Q3 GDP
Oct 29, %ann’d, Last: 2.2%, Mkt f/c: 3.0%, WBC f/c: 3.1%
The US economy continues to grow at a pace well above trend.
This outperformance is almost entirely due to the strength of
investment in data centres and other AI related assets. The
pipeline for this activity remains strong, limiting near-term
risks to the downside.
Abstracting from AI investment’s contribution, other business
investment and residential investment is weak, weighed down

by high term interest rates and uncertainty related to trade
policy. Household consumption growth is volatile month-to-
month, but the trend is solid. Absent a marked deterioration
in the labour market, which is not our expectation, this
momentum is likely to persist, as wealth continues to grow and
household income gains neutralises inflation. Come 2027 and
2028, growth is expected to slow to trend, but not below.

TUẦN 12-16/10 [Điểm nhấn thị trường] 
VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -29.18 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh 
khoản suy giảm

[Nhận định xu hướng] 
VN-Index có thể hồi kỹ thuật từ cạnh dưới tam giác, song việc chớm thủng hỗ trợ 
1.710 điểm và số mã còn giữ xu hướng tăng từ đáy tháng 7 suy gi ảm khiến xu 
hướng ngắn hạn trở nên trung tính.

### Page 2

2

Điểm nhấn thị trường 
Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -29.18 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh khoản suy 
giảm. Dầu khí, Bảo hiểm và Dịch vụ phân phối là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt nhất 
trong tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -3,013 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã MCH, VIC và FPT; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở mức 
887 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại đảo chiều bán ròng -2,021 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần 
qua và đang duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế -4,384 hợp đồng. Tự doanh đảo chiều bán ròng -
4,275 hợp đồng và đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -7,815 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam tiếp tục điều chỉnh tuần thứ tư liên tiếp, khi VN-Index lùi về đóng cửa 1.706 
điểm - giảm khoảng 1,7% so với tuần trước và đang dao động quanh vùng hỗ trợ tâm lý 
1.700 điểm, cũng là vùng đáy cũ của tháng 8. Thanh khoản thị trường tiếp tục suy yếu, 
phản ánh tâm lý thận trọng cao độ trong bối cảnh thị trường thiếu vắng thông tin hỗ trợ 
trong nước, trong khi các yếu tố bên ngoài như xung đột tại Iran, và chi phí vốn toàn cầu 
chưa hạ nhiệt tiếp tục đè nặng tâm lý nhà đầu tư. Khối ngoại duy trì chuỗi bán ròng kéo 
dài nhiều phiên liên tiếp, nối dài đà rút vốn đã lên tới xấp xỉ 95 nghìn tỷ đồng từ đầu năm. 
Trong một, hai tuần tới, mùa báo cáo kết quả kinh doanh quý 3 được kỳ vọng là chất xúc 
tác giúp thị trường giao dịch cân bằng trở lại.

Đỏ  Khả năng giảm điểm cao Bán mạnh

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX tiếp tục hình thành mẫu nến giảm điểm tiêu cực trên đồ thị tuần, đánh dấu tuần 
giảm thứ 4 liên tiếp, cho thấy áp lực phân phối vẫn đang có phần lấn át. Trên khung ngày, 
tuy chỉ số đã có nỗ lực phục hồi trở lại trong 3 phiên đan xen, đặc biệt là phản ứng trong 
phiên cuối tuần khi chạm trendline trung hạn, nhưng việc chỉ có thể hình thành các mẫu 
nến "spinning" hàm ý đà tích lũy chưa thực sự thuyết phục. Trong một vài phiên kế tiếp, 
VNINDEX có thể xuất hiện nhịp hồi kỹ thuật từ vùng hỗ trợ, tương ứng với cạnh dưới tam 
giác, nhưng sau khi chớm phá vùng hỗ trợ quanh 1710 và hiện chỉ còn một số ít các mã giữ 
được xu hướng tăng tính từ đáy tháng 7, xu hướng ngắn hạn đang trở nên khá trung tính.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên) 
Kháng cự xa: 1780 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1740 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1680 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1630 (+-15)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Document 267: Cliff Notes_ getting the balance right (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ getting the balance right (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
10:46 October 16 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September Labour Force Survey. Employment surprised
to the downside, -39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force participation from August’s near-
historic high largely offset. The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August was revised up.

Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless, the take home point from the update is that the

### Page 2

labour market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs below working-age population growth.
We expect the unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter financial conditions and cost-of-
living pressures restrict activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast peak is near at
4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the communication challenge for the RBA. The
September minutes were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing on materialising

upside risks to inflation. But, with the unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption of
4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the labour market is becoming harder to defend.
Still, with a clear focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our near-term CPI forecasts in
the October Market Outlook – we still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in September, taking the annual rate up to

3.7%yr. Energy prices were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation was a touch above
consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr). Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months (0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4%
(3.1%yr) on a 0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport services and a 0.3%
increase in medical care services (2.7%yr and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release of

the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures, with the headline and core measures up
6.3%yr and 5.0%yr respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these developments, firms
across multiple districts citing tariffs, insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost
pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect further price increases as contracts/hedges
roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is enough signal in these reports to warrant a

follow-up hike at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee members believe they are
near the end of the tightening cycle and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman both
showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear bias towards an October or December decision.
Williams stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in this cycle given known information.
And Waller also showed confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are doing some of the

FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating
that one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target.
However, as discussed in this month’s Market Outlook, capacity pressures are likely to see inflation
consistently above target, rather than below, in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity
in the housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to remain modestly restrictive over

the forecast horizon and will also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade account. At USD133.5bn, the September
result was a whisker inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More impressively, throughout
2025 and 2026 year-to-date the surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the average of
2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth of export growth is as impressive as the headline

annual gain of 36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese investment in
neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time, both trade and income receipts from offshore
investment should buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound foreign investment should
moderate the pace of gains against other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.

### Page 3

Related articles
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the evolution of producer prices across
Asia, and the likelihood of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent decades
continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was one of the key drivers of declining goods prices.
While still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing development to instead be driven by
capital, technology and energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking production are

also increasingly prioritising resilience, security and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore
more probably future supply-chain developments will help contain rising costs through productivity than
drive sustained price declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.

Document 292: Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14)

EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.
SOURCE: S&P GLOBAL, BNP PARIBAS

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

Document 320: Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

13028742     1

Economy 
Insurance costs hit the brakes 
16 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Property insurance (e.g., auto, homeowners, renters) payment growth has slowed considerably, according to Bank of America 
payments data. Median payments rose just 1% year-over-year (YoY) in 2026, easing further from the 6% growth seen in 2025, 
after a roughly 12% increase in 2024, suggesting that many consumers are getting some relief from rising insurance payments. 
• However, the moderation is uneven across consumers. Bank of America payments data suggests that Gen Z continues to 
experience insurance payment growth of nearly 6%, while increases for other generations are closer to 1% to 2%. Payments 
also rose nearly 1.5% for higher-income households but edged lower for those with lower incomes. 
• Lower accident rates, easing vehicle prices, and softer major-purchase growth may be reducing cost pressures, but geography 
remains a major dividing line. Florida metros recorded declines of roughly 7% to 8%, while California and several Northeastern 
metros continued to see relatively strong increases.

Insurance payments stabilize after years of painful growth 
The burden of higher property (e.g., auto, renters, homeowners) insurance payments may be easing for some. Notably, while the 
average payment increased 3% year-over-year (YoY), the median payment was only up 1%, according to Bank of America 
payments data. This suggests that most consumers have only seen a small increase in their premiums, though there may be a 
few outliers (Exhibit 1).  
The latest data stands in contrast to just two years ago in 2024, when most consumers saw their insurance payments surge 
about 12% YoY, after a nearly 8% YoY increase in 2023.   
Exhibit 1: Insurance payments have increased only modestly through August of this year, with growth peaking in 2024 
Median and average insurance payments (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Insurance payments by age show a similar pattern, with much stronger growth in 2024 across most generations followed by a 
cooling since then (Exhibit 2). Gen Z is the outlier here, with their payments still growing at a healthy rate, increasing almost 6% 
YoY for both 2026 and 2025, after a 7% increase in 2024. Meanwhile, Millennials and Baby Boomers experienced a 2% YoY 
increase so far this year, with Gen X only seeing around a 1% rise.

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Bank of America payments data by income provides even more insight. Higher-income households paid nearly 1.5% more in 
2026 than they did a year ago, while middle-income households saw only modest increases or none at all (Exhibit 3). Conversely, 
lower-income households actually paid slightly less so far this year than they did last year.  
Exhibit 3: Higher-income households are still paying more for insurance compared to last year, while lower earners are paying less 
Median insurance payments by income cuts (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Fewer accidents and lower vehicles prices may be driving insurance payments down 
But why might insurance payments be declining at all? One thing to keep in mind is that insurance companies often raise rates 
to cover events that occurred in previous years to recover costs, while adjusting for changing risks.  
For example, the crash rate – the number of vehicle crashes per one million miles driven – suggests that there was a sharp rise 
in accidents in 2021, according to the Department of Transportation (Exhibit 4). Additionally, new and used vehicle prices 
increased significantly after the pandemic, according to Consumer Price Index (CPI) data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics 
(BLS) (Exhibit 5). So, insurers were not only facing more vehicle replacements due to a higher crash rate, but a higher cost to 
replace vehicles as well.

However, the crash rate mostly declined from 2021 to 2024, and the Department of Transportation reported that the number of 
accidents likely decreased again in 2025. Also, while the cost to maintain and repair vehicles has consistently grown since 2021, 
the price to replace vehicles peaked in 2022 and has eased since then – the latter likely a more important factor in insurance 
costs than the former. Additionally, while the CPI index for motor vehicle insurance has fallen 5% YoY, it’s possible that this may 
partially be due to rebates, according to BofA Global Research. So, the price figured into CPI may not be fully realized by the 
consumer.

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Another factor in the divergence between a declining CPI and rising insurance payments in our data may be the growth in the 
amount of property people are insuring now. Notably though, this growth has eased in recent years. YoY growth in both new 
home and total vehicle sales peaked in mid-2023 and has grown modestly, or even declined, since (Exhibit 6). And given that 
these major purchases are still K-shaped (read more in Autos: In gear, but the “K” is still in the headlights and On the move: 
Renovation over relocation?), it could help to explain the divergence in insurance payment growth across income cohorts.

Florida’s metros have seen the largest decline when compared to other major cities 
Examining different cities across the US also sheds light on insurance trends. Consumers in major California metros saw the 
largest increases in 2026, following near double digit growth in 2025, likely driven by widespread property damage from 
wildfires, according to BofA Global Research (Exhibit 7). Major coastal metros in the Northeast like Boston and New York also 
experienced sizable increases as well.  
Conversely, many cities throughout the Sun Belt like Houston, Nashville and Dallas saw flat or even declining insurance 
payments. BofA Global Research points to several reasons for these declines: part of the equation in this region is that the 
massive runup in the cost of property insurance and domestic migration to the region through 2024 has since eased or reversed 
course.   
Exhibit 7: California major metros have seen steady insurance payment growth, while Miami metros have seen declines 
Median insurance payments by core based statistical areas (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In Florida, legislation that went into effect in 2023, eventually made its way into insurance prices in 2025, leading to widespread 
declines in premiums. The state also has a “lookback period” of three years, where insurance profits can be deemed too high and 
a rebate is issued to consumers. It’s possible this rebate is factoring into declines this year, as some carriers only allow rebates 
upon renewals.  
The Sunshine State was a notable standout for declines, with Orlando, Tampa and Jacksonville each down around 8% YoY in 
2026, followed closely by Miami with a 7% decrease. Additionally, Bank of America payments data suggests that Jacksonville, 
Miami and Orlando all had median insurance payments above the national level in 2023, though their payments were fell below 
that in 2026 (Exhibit 8).  
Major metros in Texas also saw easing insurance payments relative to 2023, while Seattle, San Jose, and Riverside, CA are 
among the major metros that went from below the national median to above in the three-year period ending in 2026.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Insurance payments include renters, auto or home, but do not include insurance escrowed into mortgage payments or deducted from a 
paycheck.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 16 日

1/3 
MSCI、トルコ株式指数の変更を凍結し、格下げの可能性を警告 
～トルコ株式市場の混乱は長期化の様相、MSCI は改革の進捗を注視～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ トルコ株式市場では、 投資信託による株式売却圧力を背景に下落基調が続いている。MSCI は
６月、一部投資信託による低流動性銘柄への保有集中や浮動株比率の歪みに懸念を示し、当
局に情報開示や市場監視の強化を求めた。背景には、政府が国内資金をリラ建て資産に誘導
したことで、低浮動株企業の上場や投資信託への資金流入が拡大し、低流動性銘柄の株価上
昇と資金流入が相互に増幅する構造が形成されたことがある。 
○ その後、資本市場委員会（ＳＰＫ）が投資信託の保有上限を厳格化し、年末までに超過分の
解消を求めたことで資金循環が逆回転した。株価下落に伴う解約増加から、一部運用会社が
解約請求に応じられない事態となり、現金確保を目的に流動性の高い銘柄にも売りが波及し
た。 当局は資金供給の拡充や投資信託の取引停止・清算のほか、 関係者を刑事告発するなど、
早期の事態収拾に向けた取り組みを強化している。 
○ MSCI は10 月15 日、 トルコ株式指数の変更を凍結し、2027 年６月までに十分な市場改革が進
まなければ、新興国市場からフロンティア市場などへの格下げを検討する可能性を示した。
格下げとなればパッシブ投資家を中心に資金流出が加速する懸念がある。加えて、利下げ観
測に伴うリラ安から家計資金が外貨や金に流れる可能性もあり、市場混乱の長期化が実体経
済や政府の政策運営に波及するリスクに注意が必要である。

トルコ株の下落基調が続いている（図１）（注１）。背景には、現金確保を目的に投資信託が株式
の売却圧力を強めていることがある。今年６月、指数算出会社である MSCI が一部の投資信託によ
る株式保有の集中や、流動性の低い銘柄に関する警告を発したことがきっかけとなった。 
具体的には、特定の中小上場企業と密接な関係にある投資信託の保有分が、浮動株比率の推計値
を人為的に押し上げていると、海外の機関投資家から懸念が寄せられていることを明らかにした。
こうした懸念は、当該銘柄の指数構成比率を歪めることにつながる。

注１ 10 月９日付レポート「トルコ金融市場の混乱が収まらない」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666443.html）

### Page 2

2/3 
このため、同社は当局に対して、実質
的な所有者に関する詳細かつ適時の情
報開示のほか、 協調的な取引行動に対す
る強固な監視と取り締まり、 構造的に歪
められた浮動株を特定するための透明
性が高いルールに基づく枠組みの構築
を求める考えを示した。そのうえで、11
月に実施する指数見直しまでに十分か
つ具体的な進展が見られない場合、 同国
と対象銘柄の適切な取り扱いについて
協議を開始する可能性があるとした。 
こうした懸念が高まった背景には、2023 年に財務相に返り咲いたシムシェキ氏の下、 同国政府が
国内貯蓄をリラ建て資産にとどめる取り組みを進めたことがある。そのため、2021 年に導入された
為替保護型預金（ＫＫＭ：リラ安による為替下落率が預金金利を上回った場合、その差額を政府・
中銀が補填する制度）を廃止し、通常のリラ建て預金への移行を促進したほか、株式や投資信託な
どにも資金を誘導した。 
一方で、同国では高金利状態が長期化しており、政府は企業に借入ではなく、株式市場の活用に
よる資金調達の積極化を推進したため、浮動株比率が低い企業が数多く上場した。こうした銘柄は
市場で売買可能な株式数が限られ、株価が大きく変動しやすいことで、価格形成に歪みが生じた。
さらに、 その後の投資ブームを追い風に投資信託が急拡大し、 投資家にとっては株式投資の間口が
広がる一方、リスクの集中が懸念された。具体的には、投資信託に組み入れられる比較的流動性が
低い銘柄への資金流入が活発化し、 株価上昇に伴い運用収益が向上した結果、 新たな資金流入を生
む循環が形成された。 
規制機関である資本市場委員会（ＳＰＫ）は８月、投資信託の純資産に対する組入比率、発行済
株式数に対する保有割合など保有ポジションの上限を厳格化し、2026 年末までに超過分の解消を
義務付ける規制変更を発表した。具体的には、10 月末までに超過分の３分の１、11 月末までに３
分の２、12 月末までに完全解消を求めている。 
その結果、 株価を押し上げてきた資金流入の循環が逆回転し、 株価下落により投資信託の解約請
求が膨らんだ。 さらに、 複数の資産運用会社が投資家による解約請求に応じられないと発表する事
態に発展した。 流動性の低い保有資産の売却が困難になり、 現金確保を目的に流動性の高い銘柄に
売却圧力が強まったことで、市場全体に売り圧力が広がっている。 
市場の混乱を受けて、 中銀は流動性確保を目的とするレポ資金の供給額引き上げのほか、 銀行間
資金市場における銀行の借入限度額も大幅に引き上げた。ＳＰＫも、複数の投資信託の取引停止・
清算を命じたほか、関係者を刑事告発するといった動きをみせている。 
こうしたなか、MSCI は 10 月 15 日、トルコ株式指数に関する変更を凍結することを明らかにし
た。当局による市場改革を評価するためにさらなる時間が必要とし、2027 年６月までに十分な進展

Standard Chartered Bank

Charts of the week: Watching earnings and a potential ‘Blue wave’

US Big Tech earnings, mid-term elections and major central bank policy meetings are the near-term market drivers 
Consensus S&P500 index Q3 earnings estimates by sector   Chance of Democrats winning control of US House and Senate*

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered; *Polymarket probability

Editorial 
Policy, politics and earnings  
Strategy summary:  We retain our near -term defensive and 
diversified stance across assets, sectors and regions amid 
near-term uncertainty around monetary policy, politics and 
earnings. The next two weeks will be busy for major central 
banks, US political watchers and technology leaders powering 
the AI supercycle. We expect the Fed, ECB and BoJ to hold 
rates next week, but markets will focus on whether central 
banks are turning more hawkish due to the latest surge in fuel 
prices. The US mid -term elections are expected to give 
Democrats control of the House and Senate, with implications 
for taxes and the AI-led investment boom.  
Meanwhile, the consensus is expecting the US tech sector to 
report over 60% y/y earnings growth for Q3, the biggest jump 
in over two decades outside the post-COVID rebound.  Each of 
these three drivers have the potential to surprise markets.  
Big Three to hold monetary policy: We expect the Fed, ECB 
and BoJ to hold policy rates next week, after September’s 
hikes, as they assess whether higher bond yields have 
tightened financial conditions enough to offset fuel-price-driven 
inflation. We expect the Big Three to hike next in December as 
fuel prices are likely to stay elevated into early next year due to 
refining capacity constraints. Nevertheless, the Fed and ECB 
are likely to deliver less than around 60bps of rate hikes priced 
in by money markets over the next 12 months on the back of 
an eventual easing of oil and fuel prices leading to disinflation 
by H2 2027. This outlook means 3 -7-year Developed Market 
investment grade bonds currently offer attractive risk-reward.  
Opportunity in Australian bonds. Other major central banks, 
such as the Bank of England, Bank of Canada and Reserve 
Bank of New Zealand are likely to hike rates by 25bps in the 
next two weeks given their focus on taming near-term inflation. 
However, we believe the Reserve Bank of Australia (RBA) is 
close to the end of its hiking cycle, even if it hikes one last time 
in the coming months (money markets give a 60% chance of a 
hike on 3 November). Australia’s domestic labour and housing 
markets and business confidence are softening after the RBA 
hiked its policy rate four times this year to a 15 -year high. 
Meanwhile, Australia’s cash rate is 60bps higher than the US 
policy rate, supporting the AUD. We continue to see attractive 
income and FX gain opportunities from AUD corporate bonds.  
A ‘Blue Wave’ in the US?  Prediction markets strongly favour 
Democrats taking the House of Representatives and possibly 
the Senate in the 3 November US mid-term elections. While an 
opposition sweep in the mid-terms has historically delivered the 
best 12-month returns for equities  among all sc enarios, partly 
due to the resulting policy gridlock, a decisive Democratic “blue 
wave” could increase longer -term risks of higher taxes and 
uncertainty over policy towards AI investment and data centres. 
With the Senate race still close and prediction markets fa llible, 
we remain cautious on risk assets in the near term.  
Closing EU bank contingent convertible bond s (CoCos1) 
and US 10 -year go vernment bond ideas:  We lock in 2% 
gains in EU CoCos and 1.7% gain in US 10 -year government 
bond ideas amid French and US political risks (page 5-6). We 
continue to hedge any inflation risks through inflation-protected 
bonds and geopolitical risk through an Overweight in gold.  
Watching Big Tech earnings:   Upcoming Big Tech earnings 
raises uncertainty as market leadership remains concentrated 
and share -price reactions more mixed. With valuations less 
likely to drive returns, earnings delivery and sustainability of AI 
investment, funding and monet isation and broadening out of 
earnings beyond tech will be critical, raising the risk of volatility 
and sector divergence.  We remain invested but defensively 
positioned, favouring diversification across sectors and regions.  
Staying defensive and diversified. We recently upgraded the 
defensive US Healthcare sector to Overweight , while retaining 
our Overweights to US tech and materials as well as financials 
in the US, Euro area and Japan. We also downgraded the more 
volatile Communications Services sector to Neutral  and took 
profits on the semiconductor segment earlier this month before 
the latest correction. Our defensive and diversified stance 
should preserve participation in further equity upside, while 
reducing exposure to earnings disappointments and supporting 
resilience through the reporting season.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya
